========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I’m here because I keep snapping at people, and it’s gotten bad enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just me having a rough day anymore. At work, I’m an inspector, and I’m supposed to be the steady one, you know, checking things, answering questions, keeping everything by the book. But lately I go from zero to yelling before I even realize what’s happening.

There was this safety meeting, and someone said something—I don’t even remember it being that serious now—but in the moment I just felt this heat come up in me, and I started yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everybody. I could hear myself doing it and still couldn’t stop fast enough. And now the younger inspectors… they act scared to come ask me anything. They kind of hover or ask someone else first, and I can tell they’re trying not to set me off.

That’s not how I want to be. I don’t want people afraid of me. So… that’s why I’m here.

It’s mostly at work that I’ve noticed it, because that’s where it’s causing the biggest problems and where people are around me enough to see it. At work, I’m an inspector, and I’m supposed to be the steady one, you know, checking things, answering questions, keeping everything by the book. But lately I go from zero to yelling before I even realize what’s happening.\n\nThere was this safety meeting, and someone said something—I don’t even remember it being that serious now—but in the moment I just felt this heat come up in me, and I started yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everybody. I could hear myself doing it and still couldn’t stop fast enough. And now the younger inspectors… they act scared to come ask me anything. They kind of hover or ask someone else first, and I can tell they’re trying not to set me off.\n\nThat’s not how I want to be. I don’t want people afraid of me. So… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this snapping or yelling happen only at work or also when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work that I’ve noticed it, because that’s where it’s causing the biggest problems and where people are around me enough to see it. Like that safety meeting I mentioned — I just went off before I could catch myself, and afterward I felt awful because I could see how everyone was looking at me.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they don’t come up to me the same way. I guess… outside of work it’s not as obvious to me, or maybe I’m just not around people in the same pressured way. The place I really see it, where I can’t deny it, is at work.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think there is something there. It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.

A lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.

So when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.

It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"A clank from a cart or the smell of hot oil can put me right back in that bay, seeing my coworker by the machine like it’s happening in front of me all over again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.

Like if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.

And I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.

It doesn’t stay put.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay, I won’t talk about the accident if I can help it, and I quit going to the industrial-history talks I used to enjoy because the old machinery pictures set me off.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.

At work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.

And I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.

Even outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.

And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\nPatient: He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"A clank from a cart or the smell of hot oil can put me right back in that bay, seeing my coworker by the machine like it’s happening in front of me all over again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.

It can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.

I know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.

It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.

I keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.

And I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.

stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay, I won’t talk about the accident if I can help it, and I quit going to the industrial-history talks I used to enjoy because the old machinery pictures set me off.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the shift and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s a whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I don’t trust that plant or the people running it anymore, and some days I feel like I’m damaged goods who can’t even do the job I spent my life getting good at.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks or stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I keep snapping at people, like at that safety meeting when I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: Even in the break room or at rehearsal, I’m watching doors, listening for sudden machine noises, and checking who’s moving behind me because my body won’t believe I’m safe.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed so many shifts and lost so much patience with trainees that I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that machinery accident after the overnight schedule change, about 2 years and 4 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.

The biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.

I also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.

And it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I keep snapping at people, and it’s gotten bad enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just me having a rough day anymore. At work, I’m an inspector, and I’m supposed to be the steady one, you know, checking things, answering questions, keeping everything by the book. But lately I go from zero to yelling before I even realize what’s happening.\n\nThere was this safety meeting, and someone said something—I don’t even remember it being that serious now—but in the moment I just felt this heat come up in me, and I started yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everybody. I could hear myself doing it and still couldn’t stop fast enough. And now the younger inspectors… they act scared to come ask me anything. They kind of hover or ask someone else first, and I can tell they’re trying not to set me off.\n\nThat’s not how I want to be. I don’t want people afraid of me. So… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this snapping or yelling happen only at work or also when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work that I’ve noticed it, because that’s where it’s causing the biggest problems and where people are around me enough to see it. Like that safety meeting I mentioned — I just went off before I could catch myself, and afterward I felt awful because I could see how everyone was looking at me.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they don’t come up to me the same way. They sort of pause, or they ask somebody else first, like they’re afraid I’m going to snap at them. That bothers me a lot. I don’t want to be that person people tiptoe around. I can feel myself getting sharp with people, and sometimes it comes out as yelling before I’ve had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Does the snapping or yelling also happen when you're with family or friends, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I guess… outside of work it’s not as obvious to me, or maybe I’m just not around people in the same pressured way. The place I really see it, where I can’t deny it, is at work.\n\nI keep snapping at people there. Like I said, that safety meeting — I started yelling before I could stop myself. It was like my mouth got ahead of me, and by the time I realized how loud I was, everybody was already staring. And now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me things, like they’re bracing for me to go off. That’s the part that really gets to me, because I don’t want people feeling that way around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you aware of any particular thoughts or concerns that are on your mind when you feel yourself getting sharp or ready to snap at someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is something there. It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\nPatient: He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.

It can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.

I see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.

At work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.

And now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.

And with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I keep snapping at people, and it’s gotten bad enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just me having a rough day anymore. At work, I’m an inspector, and I’m supposed to be the steady one, you know, checking things, answering questions, keeping everything by the book. But lately I go from zero to yelling before I even realize what’s happening.\n\nThere was this safety meeting, and someone said something—I don’t even remember it being that serious now—but in the moment I just felt this heat come up in me, and I started yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everybody. I could hear myself doing it and still couldn’t stop fast enough. And now the younger inspectors… they act scared to come ask me anything. They kind of hover or ask someone else first, and I can tell they’re trying not to set me off.\n\nThat’s not how I want to be. I don’t want people afraid of me. So… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this snapping or yelling happen only at work or also when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work that I’ve noticed it, because that’s where it’s causing the biggest problems and where people are around me enough to see it. Like that safety meeting I mentioned — I just went off before I could catch myself, and afterward I felt awful because I could see how everyone was looking at me.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they don’t come up to me the same way. They sort of pause, or they ask somebody else first, like they’re afraid I’m going to snap at them. That bothers me a lot. I don’t want to be that person people tiptoe around. I can feel myself getting sharp with people, and sometimes it comes out as yelling before I’ve had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Does the snapping or yelling also happen when you're with family or friends, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I guess… outside of work it’s not as obvious to me, or maybe I’m just not around people in the same pressured way. The place I really see it, where I can’t deny it, is at work.\n\nI keep snapping at people there. Like I said, that safety meeting — I started yelling before I could stop myself. It was like my mouth got ahead of me, and by the time I realized how loud I was, everybody was already staring. And now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me things, like they’re bracing for me to go off. That’s the part that really gets to me, because I don’t want people feeling that way around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you aware of any particular thoughts or concerns that are on your mind when you feel yourself getting sharp or ready to snap at someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is something there. It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\nPatient: He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nI see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family or friends due to your irritability or snapping at people?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nI see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family or friends due to your irritability or snapping at people?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t do as much as I used to, but I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly.

Some things I’ve stopped doing, but it feels more like I’m avoiding certain situations than that I don’t care about anything anymore. Like, I still think I would enjoy some things if I could get myself there and if I knew I wasn’t going to be uncomfortable. But it’s hard to separate what I’ve lost interest in from what just feels like too much trouble now.

I guess I haven’t really sat down and thought about it that way. There are things I used to make time for that I don’t anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy them or because I just don’t want to deal with what might come up. So… I’m not sure.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I keep snapping at people, and it’s gotten bad enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just me having a rough day anymore. At work, I’m an inspector, and I’m supposed to be the steady one, you know, checking things, answering questions, keeping everything by the book. But lately I go from zero to yelling before I even realize what’s happening.\n\nThere was this safety meeting, and someone said something—I don’t even remember it being that serious now—but in the moment I just felt this heat come up in me, and I started yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everybody. I could hear myself doing it and still couldn’t stop fast enough. And now the younger inspectors… they act scared to come ask me anything. They kind of hover or ask someone else first, and I can tell they’re trying not to set me off.\n\nThat’s not how I want to be. I don’t want people afraid of me. So… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this snapping or yelling happen only at work or also when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work that I’ve noticed it, because that’s where it’s causing the biggest problems and where people are around me enough to see it. Like that safety meeting I mentioned — I just went off before I could catch myself, and afterward I felt awful because I could see how everyone was looking at me.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors, they don’t come up to me the same way. They sort of pause, or they ask somebody else first, like they’re afraid I’m going to snap at them. That bothers me a lot. I don’t want to be that person people tiptoe around. I can feel myself getting sharp with people, and sometimes it comes out as yelling before I’ve had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Does the snapping or yelling also happen when you're with family or friends, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I guess… outside of work it’s not as obvious to me, or maybe I’m just not around people in the same pressured way. The place I really see it, where I can’t deny it, is at work.\n\nI keep snapping at people there. Like I said, that safety meeting — I started yelling before I could stop myself. It was like my mouth got ahead of me, and by the time I realized how loud I was, everybody was already staring. And now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me things, like they’re bracing for me to go off. That’s the part that really gets to me, because I don’t want people feeling that way around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you aware of any particular thoughts or concerns that are on your mind when you feel yourself getting sharp or ready to snap at someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is something there. It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\nPatient: He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nI see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family or friends due to your irritability or snapping at people?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t do as much as I used to, but I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly.\n\nSome things I’ve stopped doing, but it feels more like I’m avoiding certain situations than that I don’t care about anything anymore. Like, I still think I would enjoy some things if I could get myself there and if I knew I wasn’t going to be uncomfortable. But it’s hard to separate what I’ve lost interest in from what just feels like too much trouble now.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought about it that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Most nights, honestly.

It’s not just a little trouble sleeping. I’ll go to bed because I’m exhausted, and then I just… lie there. I’m watching the clock, hearing every sound in the house, waiting for my body to settle down, and it doesn’t. Sometimes I don’t fall asleep until it’s almost dawn, and then it feels pointless because I have to get back up.

Other nights I do fall asleep, but then I jerk awake from dreams. They have the line alarms in them, that sharp alarm sound, and that awful metal noise — the clank — and I wake up like it’s happening right then. My heart is going, and I’m sitting up before I even know where I am.

And even if I do get a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up feeling like I’ve been working all night or bracing myself all night. Like my body never actually shut off. So yes… sleep has been pretty bad.

It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is.
