I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nI see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family or friends due to your irritability or snapping at people?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t do as much as I used to, but I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly.\n\nSome things I’ve stopped doing, but it feels more like I’m avoiding certain situations than that I don’t care about anything anymore. Like, I still think I would enjoy some things if I could get myself there and if I knew I wasn’t going to be uncomfortable. But it’s hard to separate what I’ve lost interest in from what just feels like too much trouble now.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought about it that way. There are things I used to make time for that I don’t anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy them or because I just don’t want to deal with what might come up. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just a little trouble sleeping. I’ll go to bed because I’m exhausted, and then I just… lie there. I’m watching the clock, hearing every sound in the house, waiting for my body to settle down, and it doesn’t. Sometimes I don’t fall asleep until it’s almost dawn, and then it feels pointless because I have to get back up.\n\nOther nights I do fall asleep, but then I jerk awake from dreams. They have the line alarms in them, that sharp alarm sound, and that awful metal noise — the clank — and I wake up like it’s happening right then. My heart is going, and I’m sitting up before I even know where I am.\n\nAnd even if I do get a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up feeling like I’ve been working all night or bracing myself all night. Like my body never actually shut off. So yes… sleep has been pretty bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That happens, and it’s… it’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like just remembering.

It can be something small, like a cart hitting something and making that clank sound — that metal-on-metal kind of noise — and suddenly I’m not standing where I am anymore, not really. I mean, I know part of me knows I’m there, but it feels like I’m right back in that bay. Same feeling in my chest, same panic, like everything is happening all over again.

The smell of hot oil does it too. If I catch that smell, even for a second, it’s like my whole body reacts before I’ve had time to think. I see my coworker by the machine again, like it’s right there in front of me. Not like a regular memory where you think, “Oh, I remember that.” It’s more like I’m back in it, and I can’t get away from what I’m seeing.

Sometimes I’ll be standing there trying to tell myself, “You’re not there, this isn’t happening now,” but it doesn’t feel true in that moment. It feels real. Too real.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I keep snapping at people, and it’s gotten bad enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just me having a rough day anymore. But lately I go from zero to yelling before I even realize what’s happening.\n\nThere was this safety meeting, and someone said something—I don’t even remember it being that serious now—but in the moment I just felt this heat come up in me, and I started yelling. It was like my mouth got ahead of me, and by the time I realized how loud I was, everybody was already staring. And now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me things, like they’re bracing for me to go off. That’s the part that really gets to me, because I don’t want people feeling that way around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you aware of any particular thoughts or concerns that are on your mind when you feel yourself getting sharp or ready to snap at someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is something there. It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\nPatient: He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nI see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family or friends due to your irritability or snapping at people?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t do as much as I used to, but I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly.\n\nSome things I’ve stopped doing, but it feels more like I’m avoiding certain situations than that I don’t care about anything anymore. Like, I still think I would enjoy some things if I could get myself there and if I knew I wasn’t going to be uncomfortable. But it’s hard to separate what I’ve lost interest in from what just feels like too much trouble now.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought about it that way. There are things I used to make time for that I don’t anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy them or because I just don’t want to deal with what might come up. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just a little trouble sleeping. I’ll go to bed because I’m exhausted, and then I just… lie there. I’m watching the clock, hearing every sound in the house, waiting for my body to settle down, and it doesn’t. Sometimes I don’t fall asleep until it’s almost dawn, and then it feels pointless because I have to get back up.\n\nOther nights I do fall asleep, but then I jerk awake from dreams. They have the line alarms in them, that sharp alarm sound, and that awful metal noise — the clank — and I wake up like it’s happening right then. My heart is going, and I’m sitting up before I even know where I am.\n\nAnd even if I do get a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up feeling like I’ve been working all night or bracing myself all night. Like my body never actually shut off. So yes… sleep has been pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident in a very vivid way, like you're back in the moment it happened?\n\nPatient: Yes. That happens, and it’s… it’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like just remembering.\n\nIt can be something small, like a cart hitting something and making that clank sound — that metal-on-metal kind of noise — and suddenly I’m not standing where I am anymore, not really. Same feeling in my chest, same panic, like everything is happening all over again.\n\nThe smell of hot oil does it too. Not like a regular memory where you think, “Oh, I remember that.” It’s more like I’m back in it, and I can’t get away from what I’m seeing.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing there trying to tell myself, “You’re not there, this isn’t happening now,” but it doesn’t feel true in that moment. It feels real. Too real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.

At work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed a lot of shifts. Too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the younger inspectors or trainees. I used to be someone they trusted to help train people, to walk them through what to look for and how to stay careful, and I was proud of that. I liked being the person who could say, “Okay, slow down, check it again, here’s what you missed.”

But lately I snap quicker, or I get overwhelmed, or I just can’t stay steady enough with them. And people notice. They don’t say it in a cruel way, but I know I’m not being relied on the same way anymore. I’m not really trusted to mentor the younger inspectors like I was before, and that hurts. It makes me feel like I’ve lost a part of who I was at work.

And outside of work… I’ve pulled back from community theater. That used to be something I really cared about. Rehearsals, being around people, having that routine — it meant a lot to me. But now I can’t reliably get through rehearsals. I’ll think I can go, and then I can’t, or I get there and I’m not really present the way I need to be. So I stopped committing, because I don’t want to be the person everyone is waiting on or working around.

So yes, it’s affected both. Work and relationships, too, in the sense that I’m not showing up the way I used to.

And now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me things, like they’re bracing for me to go off. That’s the part that really gets to me, because I don’t want people feeling that way around me.\n\nDoctor: Are you aware of any particular thoughts or concerns that are on your mind when you feel yourself getting sharp or ready to snap at someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there is something there. It’s not always clear right in the second, but if I slow it down afterward, it’s like I’m already upset before the person even finishes talking.\n\nA lot of it goes back to this thought I can’t seem to get away from — that if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I’d stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep turning that over in my head. Like, I was the one who should’ve said, “No, this isn’t right, we’re not doing this,” and I didn’t do enough.\n\nSo when someone questions something now, or acts casual about a procedure, or even just says something that sounds like they’re not taking it seriously, I feel this… I don’t know, panic mixed with anger. Like I have to shut it down immediately, because if I don’t, something bad could happen again. And then it comes out as me being sharp or yelling, even if the person didn’t deserve that.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the person who got hurt and how that event has been affecting you since it happened?\n\nPatient: He was my coworker… someone I worked around all the time in that bay. I don’t even know how to explain it without sounding like I’m right back there, because that’s what happens to me. It isn’t just that I remember it in a normal way, like, “Oh, that was a bad day.” It’s more like something pulls me straight into it.\n\nLike if I hear a cart clank hard against something, or that metal-on-metal sound, my body reacts before I even understand what it is. Or the smell of hot oil — that smell from the equipment — it’ll hit me and suddenly I’m not where I am anymore, not completely. I’m back in that bay, seeing him by the machine like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nAnd I know, part of me knows I’m standing at work now, or I’m somewhere else, but it doesn’t feel that way at first. It feels real. I can see it too clearly. I can see the area, the machine, him there, and it’s like my mind won’t let it stay in the past. It just comes right up on me. That’s a big part of how it’s been affecting me since it happened. It doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations that remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work I’ll take the long way around just so I don’t have to pass the inspection bay. Even if it’s ridiculous, even if it adds time, I’ll do it. I know people probably notice, because it’s not the normal route, but I just… I can’t make myself walk by there unless I absolutely have to. Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know people tell me it wasn’t my fault, and I hear that, I really do, but it doesn’t settle it in my head.\n\nI keep going back over it, over and over, thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or if I had said, “No, stop, this doesn’t feel right,” maybe the run would’ve been stopped. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or I’ll catch that smell, hot oil from the equipment, and it’s like the room I’m in kind of drops away. I know, somewhere in my head, that I’m not actually in the inspection bay, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nI see it again. I see him by the machine, and it’s so clear it feels like it’s happening right in front of me all over again. It’s not like a thought I can just set aside. It comes over me and I’m back in that bay, and for a few seconds or longer, it’s like the accident hasn’t ended.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family or friends due to your irritability or snapping at people?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the reasons I finally agreed to come in, because I keep snapping at people, and it’s not like me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt work it’s gotten really bad. There was this safety meeting, and someone said something — I don’t even remember the exact words now, it wasn’t anything worth the reaction I had — and before I could stop myself I was yelling. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t do as much as I used to, but I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly.\n\nSome things I’ve stopped doing, but it feels more like I’m avoiding certain situations than that I don’t care about anything anymore. Like, I still think I would enjoy some things if I could get myself there and if I knew I wasn’t going to be uncomfortable. But it’s hard to separate what I’ve lost interest in from what just feels like too much trouble now.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought about it that way. There are things I used to make time for that I don’t anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy them or because I just don’t want to deal with what might come up. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just a little trouble sleeping. I’ll go to bed because I’m exhausted, and then I just… lie there. I’m watching the clock, hearing every sound in the house, waiting for my body to settle down, and it doesn’t. Sometimes I don’t fall asleep until it’s almost dawn, and then it feels pointless because I have to get back up.\n\nOther nights I do fall asleep, but then I jerk awake from dreams. They have the line alarms in them, that sharp alarm sound, and that awful metal noise — the clank — and I wake up like it’s happening right then. My heart is going, and I’m sitting up before I even know where I am.\n\nAnd even if I do get a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up feeling like I’ve been working all night or bracing myself all night. Like my body never actually shut off. So yes… sleep has been pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident in a very vivid way, like you're back in the moment it happened?\n\nPatient: Yes. That happens, and it’s… it’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like just remembering.\n\nIt can be something small, like a cart hitting something and making that clank sound — that metal-on-metal kind of noise — and suddenly I’m not standing where I am anymore, not really. I mean, I know part of me knows I’m there, but it feels like I’m right back in that bay. Same feeling in my chest, same panic, like everything is happening all over again.\n\nThe smell of hot oil does it too. If I catch that smell, even for a second, it’s like my whole body reacts before I’ve had time to think. I see my coworker by the machine again, like it’s right there in front of me. Not like a regular memory where you think, “Oh, I remember that.” It’s more like I’m back in it, and I can’t get away from what I’m seeing.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing there trying to tell myself, “You’re not there, this isn’t happening now,” but it doesn’t feel true in that moment. It feels real. Too real.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your reactions or behaviors since the accident have significantly impacted your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed a lot of shifts. Too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the younger inspectors or trainees. I used to be someone they trusted to help train people, to walk them through what to look for and how to stay careful, and I was proud of that. I liked being the person who could say, “Okay, slow down, check it again, here’s what you missed.”\n\nBut lately I snap quicker, or I get overwhelmed, or I just can’t stay steady enough with them. And people notice. They don’t say it in a cruel way, but I know I’m not being relied on the same way anymore. I’m not really trusted to mentor the younger inspectors like I was before, and that hurts. It makes me feel like I’ve lost a part of who I was at work.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve pulled back from community theater. That used to be something I really cared about. Rehearsals, being around people, having that routine — it meant a lot to me. But now I can’t reliably get through rehearsals. I’ll think I can go, and then I can’t, or I get there and I’m not really present the way I need to be. So I stopped committing, because I don’t want to be the person everyone is waiting on or working around.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected both. Work and relationships, too, in the sense that I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. All the time, even when I know, logically, nothing is happening.

Like in the break room at work, I can’t just sit with my coffee like I used to. I’m watching the doorway, watching who’s coming in and out, listening for any sudden machine noise out on the floor. If something bangs or clanks, my whole body tightens before I even know what it was. And I keep checking behind me, like if someone walks past or I hear shoes on the floor, I have to look. I can’t just let it be background noise.

It happens outside work too. At rehearsal, I’m supposed to be paying attention to the scene or the director or whoever’s talking, but part of me is tracking the exits, tracking people moving behind me, listening for anything sharp or sudden. It’s embarrassing, because I know it’s not a dangerous place. It’s rehearsal. It’s people I know. But my body doesn’t seem to believe that. It stays ready like something bad is about to happen.

And it’s exhausting, because I don’t feel like I can ever fully turn around and relax. Even in normal places, safe places, I’m still scanning.

Just seeing that area, even from a distance, makes my stomach drop.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If somebody starts bringing it up, I’ll shut down the conversation or suddenly need to go do something else. Sometimes I act like I’m busy, or I give a short answer and change the subject. It’s not that I don’t care — I care too much, I think. It just feels like if I start talking about it, I won’t be able to keep myself together.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ve changed things. I used to go to these industrial-history talks sometimes — old machinery, manufacturing history, that kind of thing. I always liked that stuff. But I stopped going, because they’d show pictures of old machines and equipment, and it would set me off. I’d be sitting there trying to look normal while inside I felt like I needed to get out of the room. So I just quit going. It’s easier to avoid it than risk having all of that come up in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories of the accident that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. They get stuck in my head in a way that doesn’t feel like regular remembering.\n\nIt can be something small that starts it, too. Like a cart clanking hard against something — that sharp metal sound — and before I even have time to think, I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil from the equipment… that one is bad. It just hits me, and suddenly I can see him by the machine again, like it’s not over, like it’s happening right there in front of me.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange, because I know I’m not actually back there. Part of me knows where I am. But the memory doesn’t stay like a memory. It comes in so clear and so fast that I can see the bay, the machine, him there… and I can’t just push it away. It’s like my mind grabs onto it and won’t let me look somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your coworker?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. I keep thinking I should’ve trusted that feeling more, or made a bigger deal out of it, even if it annoyed people or slowed everything down.\n\nAnd I know there were procedures and other people and all of that, but in my mind it keeps coming back to what I didn’t do. Like I should’ve spoken louder. I should’ve insisted. I should’ve stopped it somehow. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the accident that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are a few, and I feel like I’ve built my whole day around not running into them.\n\nThe biggest one is the inspection bay. I’ll go out of my way not to pass it, even if it means taking some ridiculous route through the building. I know it probably looks strange, and I’m sure people have noticed, but I just don’t want to see it. Even being near that area makes me feel like I need to get away from it.\n\nI also avoid talking about the accident. If someone brings it up, I’ll try to shut it down or change the subject. I don’t want the conversation to get going, because once it does, it’s like I can’t keep it at arm’s length anymore.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to those industrial-history talks I used to like — the ones with old machinery and manufacturing stuff. I used to really enjoy them, but then they’d show pictures of machines or equipment, and it would set me off. So I just quit going. It felt easier than sitting there trying to pretend I was fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Not just remembering it, but like I’m back there again.\n\nIt can happen so fast. A cart will hit something and make that clank — that hard metal-on-metal sound — and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Like really yelling. In front of everyone. I could hear myself doing it, and part of me was thinking, “Stop, you’re making it worse,” but I just kept going for a minute there.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act careful around me. Scared, almost. They used to come ask me questions all the time, because I’ve been doing this a long time and I know the line and the procedures, but now I can see them hesitate. They’ll stand there like they’re deciding whether it’s worth risking me biting their head off. That feels awful. I don’t want to be that person.\n\nAnd with family and friends, too, I get short. Somebody asks a normal question and I answer like they’ve accused me of something. Then afterward I feel terrible, but by then the damage is already done. It’s like I’m already halfway angry before the conversation even starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t do as much as I used to, but I don’t know if I’d call it “lost interest” exactly.\n\nSome things I’ve stopped doing, but it feels more like I’m avoiding certain situations than that I don’t care about anything anymore. Like, I still think I would enjoy some things if I could get myself there and if I knew I wasn’t going to be uncomfortable. But it’s hard to separate what I’ve lost interest in from what just feels like too much trouble now.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought about it that way. There are things I used to make time for that I don’t anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy them or because I just don’t want to deal with what might come up. So… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just a little trouble sleeping. I’ll go to bed because I’m exhausted, and then I just… lie there. I’m watching the clock, hearing every sound in the house, waiting for my body to settle down, and it doesn’t. Sometimes I don’t fall asleep until it’s almost dawn, and then it feels pointless because I have to get back up.\n\nOther nights I do fall asleep, but then I jerk awake from dreams. They have the line alarms in them, that sharp alarm sound, and that awful metal noise — the clank — and I wake up like it’s happening right then. My heart is going, and I’m sitting up before I even know where I am.\n\nAnd even if I do get a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up feeling like I’ve been working all night or bracing myself all night. Like my body never actually shut off. So yes… sleep has been pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the accident in a very vivid way, like you're back in the moment it happened?\n\nPatient: Yes. That happens, and it’s… it’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because it doesn’t feel like just remembering.\n\nIt can be something small, like a cart hitting something and making that clank sound — that metal-on-metal kind of noise — and suddenly I’m not standing where I am anymore, not really. I mean, I know part of me knows I’m there, but it feels like I’m right back in that bay. Same feeling in my chest, same panic, like everything is happening all over again.\n\nThe smell of hot oil does it too. If I catch that smell, even for a second, it’s like my whole body reacts before I’ve had time to think. I see my coworker by the machine again, like it’s right there in front of me. Not like a regular memory where you think, “Oh, I remember that.” It’s more like I’m back in it, and I can’t get away from what I’m seeing.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing there trying to tell myself, “You’re not there, this isn’t happening now,” but it doesn’t feel true in that moment. It feels real. Too real.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your reactions or behaviors since the accident have significantly impacted your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed a lot of shifts. Too many, really. And when I am there, I don’t have the patience I used to, especially with the younger inspectors or trainees. I used to be someone they trusted to help train people, to walk them through what to look for and how to stay careful, and I was proud of that. I liked being the person who could say, “Okay, slow down, check it again, here’s what you missed.”\n\nBut lately I snap quicker, or I get overwhelmed, or I just can’t stay steady enough with them. And people notice. They don’t say it in a cruel way, but I know I’m not being relied on the same way anymore. I’m not really trusted to mentor the younger inspectors like I was before, and that hurts. It makes me feel like I’ve lost a part of who I was at work.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve pulled back from community theater. That used to be something I really cared about. Rehearsals, being around people, having that routine — it meant a lot to me. But now I can’t reliably get through rehearsals. I’ll think I can go, and then I can’t, or I get there and I’m not really present the way I need to be. So I stopped committing, because I don’t want to be the person everyone is waiting on or working around.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected both. Work and relationships, too, in the sense that I’m not showing up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: Yes. All the time, even when I know, logically, nothing is happening.\n\nLike in the break room at work, I can’t just sit with my coffee like I used to. I’m watching the doorway, watching who’s coming in and out, listening for any sudden machine noise out on the floor. If something bangs or clanks, my whole body tightens before I even know what it was. And I keep checking behind me, like if someone walks past or I hear shoes on the floor, I have to look. I can’t just let it be background noise.\n\nIt happens outside work too. At rehearsal, I’m supposed to be paying attention to the scene or the director or whoever’s talking, but part of me is tracking the exits, tracking people moving behind me, listening for anything sharp or sudden. It’s embarrassing, because I know it’s not a dangerous place. It’s rehearsal. It’s people I know. But my body doesn’t seem to believe that. It stays ready like something bad is about to happen.\n\nAnd it’s exhausting, because I don’t feel like I can ever fully turn around and relax. Even in normal places, safe places, I’m still scanning.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
